3216 


THE GAZETTE OF INDIA : OCTOBER 29 , 1960 / KARTIK 7 , 1862 


No doubt, when it suited the Bank later to resile from the agreement, they did so , 
and the result was the entry of adverse remarks, contrary to the arrangement in 
Exts. W / 426 and 427. 

88 . The further argument is raised that there was no enforceable contract 
between the parties. I do not agree. It is permissible to spell out an agreement 
from correspondence between the parties. Exts , W / 426 and 427 do establish an 
enforceable agreement between the parties in the matter of service remarks and 
especially so , when it had been acted upon in respect of various other matters , 
referred to therein . I find accordingly . 

87 . When there was an agreement, providing for entry of remarks, only after 
Coportunity had been given to the employees concerned , to be heard in their 
defence , the management were guilty of unfair labour practice in flouting the 
agreenient, and making adverse remarks against the workman in question without 
a hearing. The argument, that the management were entitled to do so . Kecause 
they were mere annual reports made by superior officers, is without substance . 

88 . The next contention for the workman is that apart from the agreement, 
there is a provision in Paragraph 518 of the Sastry Award for the making of 
entries in service Ales . and that , this Paragraph cannot be divorced from 521, 5B 
and 7B , where for major , or minor mis - conduct , making of adverse remarks is one 
of the forms of punishment. The contention on behalf of the Bank , that the re 
marks made in scrvice Ales against the workmen in question were mera annual 
reports , and were not in the nature of adverse remarks, is of no force Some of 
the remarks at any rate , have the effect of deliberately blasting the career of the 
workinan . Especially Shri Srivastava, had been denied promotion in the past. 
His representation against his supersession had not been successful. The docu 
ments, Exts . W / 468 , to 478 , are important in this connection . When already he 
had ( omolained that he had been superseded , the further remarks , that he was an 
" unsatisfactory clerk " , and that he was " lazy " , and " unreliable " wer ; not expected 
to do him any good . On the contrary , they did him immense harm , To be called 
unsatist actory and unreliable was sufficient to mar his future career . The 
contention on behalf of the Bank , that these remarks were innocuous, and that 
they were in the nature of annual reports , and that they were not meant to stand 
in the way of the career of the employees , is unsound . The management were not 
precared to withdraw them . when the Union requested them to do so . Most of 
the employces had put in long years of service , and these remarks were certainly 
pot malcu ated to enable them to advance in their career . The workmen were 
just fcd in prot sting against the entry of adverse remarks without a hearing , and 
taxing the management with breach of faith , and deliberate violation oť the agree 
meri of June 1954 , without any valid reason . 

80 . The management have, however taken shelter behind the plea, that the said 
agreement even if true and enforceable , was not of any value after the publication 
of tlie Sastry Award . It can be by no means he contended , that terms favourable 
to the workmen were intended to go by the board . This condition about entry of 
adversa remarks only after a hearing . was a condition of service. It was in any 
care a concession and could not be altered except by the procedure prescribed 
under Section 94 of the Act . This agreement was not superseded by the award 
and did not cease to be in force, when the impunged adverse remarks were made. I 
Anri ccordingly . 

90. Even taking the Sastry Award , as it exists , and independentlv of the agree 
menl ,f June, 1954 , the making of entries by way of adverse remarks did amount 
ito a “ punishment" within the terms of Paragraph 521 ( 5 ) ( b ) , and ( 7 ) (b ) of the 
Sastry Award , and such entries could not be made without en cuiry into the con 
duct of the workmen , as laid down in the Award and justifying the making of such 
remarks . 


91 . It has been next argued that the Sastri Award ceased to be of force after 
31st March , 1950 , and especially when the workmen had giveu notice of termina 
tion . No doubt there are various matters referred to the National Tribunal in re 
gard to the Banking Industry But till a new award is publisherl or there is 
legislative enactment. The Sastry Award continues to be in force till it 
suders- ded , and it was in force at the material dates so far as this proceeding is 
concerned . 


92 . In the above circumstances the making of adverse r -marks against the work 
mon , without a hearing . is illegal and unļustified and arbitrary , and is an act of 
unfair labour practice. The workman had the right to protest against the same.

93. Next we have evidence on behalf of the workmen , that Mr, Brown and Shri 
Sárwal abused them in objectionable language. That no management has the

case referred to above. This has been sct out in the cvidence of Sarvashri Syal 
and Chakrawarti. In this connection , it was explained on behalf of the manage

izainst the Manager and Accountant under that Section . What is important to 
110te is , that in fact the workmen complained that they had been abused in

very beginning, and repeated thereafter . They made this the basis of the com 
plaint dated 4th April, 1959, demanding an enquiry under Paragcaph 517 of the 
Sastry Award .

men therein named were only temporary employees , whereas in fact they had 
been in employment from a considerable time, and that these letters were 
manufactured to deny them the privilege attached to persons, who served for 
more than six months at a stretch , according to the provisions of the Bank 
Award . It is pointed out, that Mr. Brown took charge of Delhi Office as 
Manager only in December . 1957, and that on the dates which these documents 
bear, he was not the Manager of the Delhi Branch at all. It is perfectly obvious, 
that he was not the Manager in July , and September , 1957, and these documents 
do certainly support the workmen s contention . According to the cvidence of 
Shri Syal. Mr. Brown took office in Delhi Branch in December, 1957. Before 
him Mr. Keilman was the Manager . Mr. Brown pre - dated the two letters Exts . 
546 and 547 , to show , that the employers were temporary . Shri Sval has sworn 
that these ar signed hv Mr Brown . The name of Shri Kislzen Gonal appears 
in Ext. W / 547. as " K .G ." . He was an employec of Grindlays Bank , He was the 
Typist of the Manager , Mr. Brown . The services of the emolovees of Grindys 
Bank were taken over by National Bank . The above evidence proves that 
Exts . W / 548 and 547 are ante - dated documents . This also amounts to unfair labour 
practice .

98 . It is against the background of unfair labour practice by the management, 
as set out above, that we must appraise the charge - sheets scrved on 11th April 
1959 . It may be noticed , that there is no definition of " unfair labour practice " 
in the Industrial Disputes Act, but reference may be made to the National Labour 
Relations Act of America , where there is reference to the same in Sections 7 
and 8 . There is also mention of the same in the Trade Unions Amendment Act 
of 1947 , which was however not brought into force , and in Section 28 ( k ) there 
is definition of " unfair labour practice " by an employer . The contention on 
behalf of the workmen is , that generally it is understood , that any action on 
the part of the management calculated to underminc trade union activity is unfair 
labour practice. Without accepting the extremo contention , as stated above, in 
my opinion , any action of the management, which secks to adversely affect the 
established rights of the workmen , based also upon usage, or practice , and 
amounting to conditions of service , can be fairly regarded as unfair labour 
practice. The remarks in Management Functions under Collective Bargaining 
by Ludwig Teller , Volume II , Article 381 , page 762, are also important. The 
rcmarks in Indian Cycle Manufacturing Company ( 1951 I LLJ 390 ) , and also 
in J. K . Industries ( 1951 I LLJ 44 ) , and Kanpur Omni Bus Company ( 1951 II LLJ 
483 ) are material. In Vanaspati Products ( 1953 II LLJ 851) it was held , that 
it would not be easy to have tangible evidence of unfair labour practice and it 
would be necessary to see whether the apparent cause is the real cause . In the 
case of Indian Bank ( 1953 T LLJ 230 ) it was found that importing cxtraneous 
considerations amounted to unfair labour practice . 


99 . The contention on behalf of the workmen , is that considering the inci 
dents that had happened before, and especially considering the fact, that the 
workmen had preferred a complaint to the Head Office in London , demanding 
an enquiry against Shri Sarwal and Mr. Brown , the management ietaliated by 
herving charge - shects against the worknen , with untrue allegations. There is , 
the fact that the charge - shects were served under the signature of Shri T . B . 
Sarwal. I have set out the cvidence on behalf of the workinen , which proves , 
that Shri Sarwal also abused the worlmen . There was an enquiry demanded 
again - t Shri Sarral also and a romplaint was preferred against him in the Head 
Office at London . In the explanatin : s furnished by the workmen , there are 
express Allcea ions to the effect , that they had demanded an enquiry against 
Mr. Brown and Shri Sarwal for having used abusive language and calling them 
names , and of wrongfully confining them on 31st Mørch 1959, that Shri T . B . 
Sarwal, an officer, against whom the workmen had a gricvance , and in whose 
impartiality they had no faith , had apnointed himself as an Enquiry Officer , 
that he was the man , who had made allegations against them , that this meant, 
that he was both the judge and the prosecutor , and that iſ the management 
drorecrird with the charges . They Woje doing so at their own risk . The work 
men assorted that the charghushentu lied hien issued against them out of vindic 
tives , and malice In the subsequent letters also obicctions were taken to 
Shri Sarwal issuing the charir - shorts , and being the Enquiry Omcor. Even 
Ext. M / 1 to M / 6 . the Enquiry Registet s show that this objection was being 
rencated . It is not without significance that the charges are after the pattern 
of the allevations contained in Ext , W / 377, the comnlaint prefrrred by the 
workmen , and their apprehensions as expressed therein , came out true. What 
is important to note is, that person , whom the worlomen accused as being 
biased , and whom they accused of being responsible for the incidents and using 
abusive and th - eatening language constituted himself arbiter of the destinies 
of the six workmen concerned . He issued charge -sheets , and he constituted 
himself the Enquiry Officer , and he was also the punishing authority . The work 
men were pursuing the matter with the Head Office also , as can be seen from 
the corregrondence already set out above . In the above circumstaners , it is 
perfectly obvious, that the very initiation of the disciplinary procceding was 
wrong , Surh disciplinary proceedings could not have been initiated by persons 
against phom ille worlmen had preferred a complaint in terms of Exi. W / 377, 
under Paragraph 517 of the Sastry Award. 


100. In this connection it is relevant to refer to the decision in Chowdhry Vs. 
Union of India ( 1957 I LLJ 494 ) . That shows that if the charge - sheeting and 
punishing Authority was biased , then the enquiry was vitiated , because bias goes 
to the root of the matter. One of the rules of natural justic is , that not only 
should justion be done, but also should seem to be done. It is further stated , 
that . Even thouch the principle. that the prosecutor cannot be a judge. is not 
strictly applicable to departmental enquiries, still he should not lower himself 
to the status of a common prosccutor , that is to say of a person who feels it a 
part of his function to hring the guilt home to the accused at any cost . He must 


3220 THE GAZETTE OF IND11 : OCTOBER 29 , 1960/ KARTIK 7, 1882 [PART II 

= = = = 

= = 
act with the detachment of a Judge since he is proſessing to cxercise that digni 
fied function . Thougii a departmental enquiry is not conducted with the rigi 
dity of a judicial t _ ial, still the person dealing with thc enquiry at any stage 
is in the position of a judge, and the rules of natural justice demand , that he 
should not himsell be personally interested in the case . He should be a person 
with an open mind , a mind , which is not biased against the delinquent. He 
should not have pre - judged the issue . He cannot act both as a judge and a 
witness . It is also laid down , that the Enquiry Oficer must be careful to deal 
with the charges , as framed , and not depart from them , or import cxtrancous 
matler s . 


101. Again , another decision is Mineral Development Co . ( A . I . R . 1960 
Supreme Court 468 ) . This also deals with the doctrine of bias . It was held , 
that, when bias is assumed to exist, the person , against whom this allcgation 
is made , should not take part in any decision . It is not necessary , that there 
should be actual bias or prejudice . It is sufficient if there are circumstances 
creating a rcasonable suspicion of bias. It , considering the circumstarices , it 
is not possible to assume, that the person concerned would not bear to bring a 
fair and impartial mind , it must be held , that bias exists . The same principles 
are found in Subba Rao Vs. State of Andhra Pradesh ( 1959 I LLJ 209 ) , and in 
Joti Pershad Vs. Superintendent of Police ( A . I. R . 1958 Punjob 327 ) . On the 
facts of the present case, the workmen wcre certainly entitled to have appre 
hension about this matter and entitled to impute hias to Shri Sarwul and Mr. 
Brown , especially in view of the various circumstances , prior to the institution 
of the charge - sheets and the con mencement of the enquiry on 16th April 1959 , 
and more especially , when they had also called for an enqui. y against these 
person under Paragraph 517 of the Sastry Award . Shri Salwal admits in his 
evidence, that the six employecs did take ohjection to his being the Enquiry 
Omcer , and that he could not be appointed Enquiry Officer. They also took the 
objection , that as he was to be produced as a witness , he should not be the 
Enquiry Officer . In my opinion , Shri Sarwal was not competení to issue the 
charge - sheets , or hold thc enquiry or punish the workmen . This violated onc 
of the most essential principles of natural justicc . This shows, want of goud 
faith on the part of the management. The Manger and h ( Accountant were not 
entitled to initiate the disciplinary proceedings at all especially which the work 
men had themselves preferred the complaint against them , and had remanded 
an enquiry under Paragraph 517 of the Sastry Award. I find that the initiation 
of the disciplinary proceedings is wrongful and illegal, and that this alonc is 
sufficient to yitiate the antire proceedings , and to justify the conclusion , that 
the dismissal of the wolkmon is not in goont faith and is wrongful, and thal it 
is an act of unfair labour pacticc . and victimisation for union activities, and 
that it is inal fide . 


102. It has also been contended , that Shri Sarwal could not be the Enquiry 
Officer in terms of the Bank Award . What is pleaded is , that, according to 
Paragraph 521 of the Sastry Award , the Bank should nominate the Enquiry 
Officer , and the Appellate Authority . It is the case of the Bank , that a notice

xure " E " in the statement of claim . It is signed by Mr. Brown , the Manager ,

would hear any appeal, was Mr. G . W . Brown. Some argument was advanced ,

and 547, with a view to suit the Bank s case , only after the enquiry proceed

and Shri Sarwal the Enguiry Office . The contention on hohlaf of the Union 
is , that this notification is not valid in tcrins of the award . What is provided 
in the Sastry Award is , that the " Bank " should nominate the Appellate Autho 
rity, and the Enquiry Officer It can hardly he contended , that by the word 
" Bank " is meant only the Manager of the Branch . It is not reasonable to hold ,

himself the Appcllate Authority , as thouglı hc represented the Bank . It more 
reasonable to hold the view , that by the word " Bank " is meant the Board of 
Directors , or at least the General Mannger , or at lcast the Head Office of the 
Bank . It cannot be said , that if the Delhi Branch was closed the Bank was 
closed . In this connection , it is pointed out, that in the order of reference, that 
ended in the Sastry Award , the principal office of the Bank is shown to be at 
Bombay and Calcutta . It is argued , that, in any case , so far as the Bank in

117. The above are the documents , that have been referred to before me in 
considerable detalj in regard to the enquiry against the several workmen . These 
contain the various objections raised to the Enguiry by the workmen then and 
there , and we hav . also their sworn testimony , that the Enquiry was not just or 
fuir . It scems to me, that the contention on behalf of the workmen , that the 
enquiry held by the Enquiry Officer is vitiated in more ways than one , and that 
it is not a fair and impartial enquiry according to the principles of natural justice 
must be accepted , and upheld . It is well established on the evidence , that 
Objections were being taken at almost every stage to Shri Sarwal being the 
Enquiry Officer and want of confidence was expressed in him . This was not un 
warranted in view of the complaint against him , and the Manager, and in view 
of the demand for enquiry against them under Paragraph 517 of the Sastry Award . 
Such demand for Enquiry against the Officers had been put forward on a previous 
occasion in the matter of the incident relating to Messrs James , Bottrill, and 
Harvey. The contention on behalf of the workmen is, that in view of the demand 
for Enquiry against Mr. Brown and Mr, Sarwal they had both become vindictive , 
and that they persisted in the inquiry against the six workmen , in spite of its 
being pointed out, that the initiation of the enquiry itself was illegal. It is 


- 


- - 


SFC . 3 ( ii ) ] THE GAZETTE OF INDIA : OCTOBER 29 , 1960 / KARTIK 7 , 1882 3231 

- - - - 
necessary to re - call the observations in Mineral Developnient Limited vs. State 
of Bihar ( A . I. R . 1960 Supreme Court 468 ) at pagc 472 . It is stated as follows: --- 


" Tribunals or authorities, who are entrusted with quasi- judicial functions 

are as much bound by the relevant principles , governing the doctrine 
of bias as any other judicial tribunal. This Court in a recent decision 
in Nageswara Rao V . The Sials of Andhra Pradesh ( A . I. R . 19159 
S . C . 376 ) observed as follows: 


" The principles governing the " doctrine of blas" ris -a - l is judicial tri 

bunals are well settled and they are ; ( i ) no man shall be a judge 
in his own cause ; ( ii ) justice should not only be done but mani 
festly and undoubtedly seem to be done. The two maxims yield 
the result that if a member of a judicial body is subject to a bias 
( whether financial or other ) in favour of, or against, any party to 
a dispute , or is in such a position that a bias must be assumed to 
cxist, he ought 110t to take part in the decision or sit on the 
tribunal, and that any direct pecuniary interest, however small, in 
the subject matter of inquiry will disqualify a judge, and any 
interest , though not pccuniary , will have the same effect, if it is 
Sufflciently substantial to create a reasonable suspicion of bias" . 
The said principles are cqually applicable to other authorities, 
though they are not courts of justice or judicial tribunals , who 
have to act judicially in deciding the rights of others , i.e . authorities 
who are empowered to dischargc " quasi- judicial functions" . As 
observed in the above -mentioned decision , the question would be , 
whether in the present casc Shri Sarwal, who was the Enquiry 
Ollicer of the Bank had personal bias against the Several work 
mon . Secondly , it will be necessary to scrutinise the record , and 
ascertain , whether reasonable opportunity was given to the work 
men to show cause , or wh - ther they were cienied that right. I 
have no doubt that the evidence establishes conslusively , that tlie 
Enquiry Officer and the Manager had personal bias against the six 
workmen now in question , on account of their Union activities and 
On account of their demanding an enquiry under Paragraph 517 of 
the Sastry Award . This was sufficient io disqualify the Enquiry 
Oficer froin proceeding with the enquiry . It ought to be seen , 
that it was the Enquiry Officer , who issued the cliarge - sheets . 
He was the person who conducted the proceedings Ile was the 
person who is said to have recorded the statements of the witnesses , 
examined on hehalf of the management. He was the person , who 
held the workmın guilty of the charge againsự thom in his letter 
dated 17th June, 1959. He asked the workmen to show cause 
against the proposed punishment, and he advised the punishment 
of dismissal. Ultimately he signed the order of dismissal, dismis 
sing the workmen . The record shows, that he was the person who 
did every thing for the Bank from start to finish . It is important 
to note the evidence of Shri Sarwal in this conncction . He deposes , 
that he signed the charge - sheets , that he was the Enquiry Officer 
in all these cases , and inat he was the unthority 10 inflict punish 
ment on all the workmen . There was 110 - one to represent the Bank 
at the timc of enquiry . Nonc represented the case on behalf of 
the Bank . He called the witnesses. He put them questions, and 
took down their statements. The witnesses were selected by him 
at the enquiry . Therefore , he was not only the Enquiry Officer , 
and the punishing authority but also the representative of the 
Bank , who conducted the proceedings , selecter witnesses , called 
them , and recorded their statements. It seems to me, that it was 
highly undesirable that a person who had been accused of bias, and 
against whom an enquiry had been demanded should have con 
ducted the proccedings in the above manner . This was a flagrant 
violation of the law relating to observance of the principles of 
natural justice , as laid down in the decision referred to above.

125 . On the other side, we have the evidence of Shri Suraj Parkash , MW 4. 
He says , that he does not know of any reports against him by the employees . 
He never used to go to their houses, and harass them and their family members . 
He never went with any bad characters to the employees houses . In my opinion , 
the evidence on behalf of the employees is nearer the truth . The witnesses, 
WWS, 3 and 4 , gave their evidence in a straightforward manner , and they aro

THE GAZETTE OF INDIA : OCTOBER 29, 1960 / KARTIK 7, 1882

not shown to be interested in any manner in Shri Malhotra and I believe them . 
Comment has however been made on the fact, that both WWS, 3 and 4 , claim to 
have put the date in the document. This may be due to a buna fide m stake, and 
want of recollection , and therefrom it cannot be contended , thai their evidence 
is untrue , or that the document has bcen manufactured for the occasion . On the 
other hand , it cannot be over - looked , that it was no part of the duty of a Notary 
Public to assist in a managerial enquiry , and it is rather surprising , that a Notary 
Public s services were engaged in a domestic enquiry. Shri Suraj Parkash has 
admitted , that he was paid by the Bank at the rate of Rs. 150 per day . He has 
been the Bank s Notary Public from 1958 . I am not able to say, that he is such & 
dis - interested witness as he is made out to be. In fact, Shri Malhotra himselt 
complained in his letter, which I have already referred to above , about this inci . 
dent. It cannot be said , that this incident was invented for the purpose of this 
casc . On the contrary , a number of communications were addressed to the 
management about the use of undesirable elements against the concerned work 
men , namely Exts . W / 15 , 69, 127, 311. It is in the cvidence of the worsmen , 
that undesirable elements were found moving about also in the Bank premises , 
The evidence of Shri Sarwal is , that the employees wrote to him , complaining 
against thc Notary Public , and his conduct. He could not take any action . He was 
a public ofcial. He admits , that another Notary Public could have been en 
gaged . He also admits , that the Bank could have asked Shri Şuraj Parkash not 
to come, but he continued right through the enquiry . When his attention is drawn 
to Ext. W / 138, a complaint of intimidation by Mr. Kapoor, he says that he did 
not take any action as he did not believe it and it was upto the concerned work 
man to take such steps, as were available to him . Shri Sarwal says, that he took 
the Notary Public s assistance to have letters delivered . I do not believe, that 
the assistance stopped there. I have no doubt, that, as charged by the work 
men , he was gdying guidance and assistance to Shri Sarwal in the Enquiry . Shri 
Suraj Parkash says, that a Notary s functions are to attest, verify , and certify 
documents , and discharge other functions under the Act. He can be asked to 
attest/ delivery of letters, as per the Act. Nothing has been cited before me to 
show , that it is a part of the Notary Public s duty to take part in proceedings 
before domestic tribunals . I do not believe that it was his function to reman 
with the Enquiry Officer in a domestic enquiry , and guide him and assist him in 
the matter of the said enquiry . Shri Suraj Parkash admits , that the workmen 
did raise objection to his presence . He said , that, if the Enquiry Offlcer did not 
want him , he would go out. Shri , Sarwal states, that the employees requested , 
that they might be permitted to bring a lawyer. They wanted him to permit anothr 
Notary Public to be present at the enquiry proceeding , but he did not give per + 
mission Exts. W /63 and 64 have also been relied upon as the letters written 
in this connection . It seems to me, that this conduct of the Enquiry Officer is 
also an indication of bias. If he thought a Notary Public was necessary in the 
Interests of the management. i ! was only fair and reasonable , that the employees 
also should have been permitted to have their Notary Public , especially when 
complaints had been laid against the conduct of Mr. Suraj Parkash , as proved by 
Ext. W /645 , and the evidence of WWS 3 and 4 . I do not attach any weight 
to the certificates given at the end of the proceedings in Exts . M / 1 - 6 , that the 
proceedings took -place in the manner recorded therein , on the several dates , Shri 
Suraj Parkash was prepared to put his seal and initials to anything which Shri 
Sarwal wrote , and to anything that was required by the Enquiry Officer and 
in whatever manner he was required to do, so especially when he was in the pay 
of the Bank . The fact , that Shri Suraj Parkash was associated with the enquiry , 
is also an indication of bad faith , I do not belicve his evidence , or the certificate 
attached in the enquiry proceedings, or the evidence of Shri Sarwal in this con 
nection , about the manner in which the enquiry was conducted , I prefer to be 
lieve the evidence on behalf of the workmen . The above circumstances also show . 
that the enquiry was neither fair , nor impartial. 


126 . Yet another circumstance arises with reference to the events that took 
place from 17th June, 1959. As already pointed out on 17th June, 1959, the work 
men were informed , that the Enquiry Oncer had come to the conclusion , that 
the charges were proved . There are typed findings , as representing the findings 
arrived at by the Enquiry Omcer on that date . In Ext. M / 1 there are proceedings 
dated 22nd June, 1959, and these are signed by Shri Parvana. But it is important 
to remember the evidence on behalf of the workmen that there were blank 
spaces in the reglsters . The signature of Mr. Parvana on 22nd Jun , 1959, proves 
only that what was written in the hand of Mr. Sarwal on 22nd June, 1959 , above 
the signature was in existence. It does not necessarily prove that the findings 
were in cxistence or that the loose shects had been pasted in the books by that 
time. The important point to be noticed is , that copies of these findings were 
not supplied to the workmen in spite of their request . They were entitled to

copies of the findings , especially when they had been called upon to put - forth cir 
cumstances in mitigation of pushment. I am convinced , and I belicve , that the 
typed Andings now found in the enquiry books, were not in existence on 17th June,

findings in respect thereof, on 17th June, 1959. 

127. The next circumstance to be noticed is , that in the findings it has been 
stated , that the various workman - had been given an opportunity of a hearing 
with regard to the nature of the punishment, and that the workmen were directed 
to appear before the Enquiry Officer on 22nd June, 1959. It is important to note , 
that there is nothing in the find ngs about the nature of the proposed punish 
ment. It will be noticed , that in the Bank Award in Paragraph 521 there are 
different kinds of pun shment to be imposed on the workmen . There is dis 
tinction between major mis - conduct and minor mis - conduct. Even for major 
mis - conduct, one of the forms of punishment proposed is the entry of adverse 
remarks. Holding of meetings in the Bank premises without authorisation is 
treated as minor mis - conduct, in Para 521 ( 6 ) ( h ) of the Bank Award . What is 
important to note is , that , according to the Sastry Award , it was imperative, that 
the proposed pun shment should be communicated to the workmcn , so that they 
may show cause against thc punishment, and also plcad mitiga ing circumstances 
in respect of the same. The fact, that the nature of the proposed punishment is 
not disclosed in the letter dated 17 h June, 1959, is a serious omission and is 
against the provisions of the Award . In such circumstances, it was not possible 
for the employees to plead anything in mitigation of the proposed punishment, 
when the nature of the punishment was not disclosed . 

128 . Next we come to the events of 23rd . I have already referred to the 
evidence of Shri Sarwal, that he read something from pencil notes , on which 
he is not able to lay his hands nuw . The contention on behalf of the workmen 
is , that Shri Sarwal pronounced the punishment on 23rd June , 1959. For example 
if we takt Ext. M / 1 , the record of enquiry against Shri Lajpat Rai Mulhoira , 
after the entrics on 22nd June, 1959 , there is an entry , the heading of which is 
" punishment” . It is relevant to state , that at the end of the note at page 48 , the 
following sentence occurs : 


" I have no option , but to consider , that he is unft for retention in the 

Bank s service any longer, and that he should be dismissed with 
inmediate effect." 


On 23rd there is a typed memo., and the gist of it is, that there was no mitiga 
tion or cxtenuat ng circumstance in the case of Shri Malhotra , that he along with 
the other " charges " walked out of the enquiry room , and shouted slogans, and 
that he advised Shri Parvana , that no adjournment could be given . But thcre 
is a note again on 2nd July, 1959, that the Enquiry Officer advised Shri Malhotra 
and Shri Parvana , that he would communicate his decision regarding punish 
ment in writing at 5 P . M ., or as soon thereafter as possible . I havc already re 
ferred to the letters , wherein the workmen wrote , that they reported themselves 
for duty on 27th Jurie , 1959. In Paragraph 321 ( 9 ) of the Award it is mentioned , 
that if it was decided to take any disciplinary action against an employee , 
such decision should be communicated within 3 days . The contention on behall 
of the workmen is, hat, because the order of the management, dismissing them 
Irom service , was not communicated within 3 days of 23rd June , 1959 , they 
reported themselves for duty on 27th Junc, 1959. I have no doubt at all, that 
in fact Shri Sarwal pronounced thc punishment of dism ssal on 23rd June, 1959. 
The management however did not comply wh the reguirements of Paragraph 
521 ( 9 ) , and this prompted the workman to send their letters dated 27th June, 
1959. It is perfectly clear, that, in view of this lotter, the Bank thought up 
of an excusc , and sent a lotter dated 24 / 27th June, 1959, in which it was made 
to appear , that Shri Parvana had asked for an adjournment on 23rd June, 1959, 
and that, therefore, the matter was adjourned to a subsequent date. This was 
merely an excuse to enabl, the Bank to get -over their omission to observe the 
directions in Paragraph 521 ( 9 ) . This was done in order to make it appear , 
that the actual order of disinisial was passed only on 2nd July , 1959 , and that 
it was communica ed in accordance with the terms of Paragraph 521 ( 9 ) . It is 
not without sifnificance , that the letters bear the date 24th , though actually 
they were not despatched earlier than 27th . This very fact gives an index 
As to the truth in the matter. It has been contended, not without force on

131. From the evidence, above referred to , it is clear , that the Enquiry Officer 
proceeded ex - parte against Shri Devi Pershad on 16th May 1959, and on 29th

the refusal as endorsed on Ext. M / 48 . The cvidence of Shri Devi Pershad is, 
that he sent several letters, requesting for permission to cross - examine Shri Roy

cause against the punishment. He never refused any letters inside the Bank 
premisca . He says, with reference to Ext. M / 50 , that he cannot remember, the 
de e when this letter was brought to his house . His letter , Ext. M / 52 was 
written to the Manager . His mother told him on 3rd June, that the Postman had 
refused to deliver a registered letter to her. He denies, that he refused the 
letter , Ext. M /69, and states that he does not know Urdu or what endorsement 
had been wri ton on the letter . In the absence of the evidence of the persone 
who are alleged to have made the endorsement on Ext. M / 46 , it is not possible 
to believe , that Shri Devi Pershad refused the letter . It is also not easy to 
understand as to how , when he was under suspension , he went to the Bank on 
28th May 1959. The explanation that he went to the Bank for subsistence 
allowance cannot be easily believed . It is not without significance , that, even 
the endors ment, referred to above is dated 29th May 1959, and not 20th May 
1959, on which date the letter is said to have been tendered . I am unable to hold 
that Shri Devi Pershad had notice of the enquiry on 16th May 1959, or 29th May 
1959. The Enquiry Officer was not entitled to come to the conclusion , on the 
evidence recorded behind the back of the workman suddenly on 17th June 1959, 
that the charges had been prove against the workman without his being given 
an opportunity in his defence. The Enquiry Officer proceeded , even without car 
ing to ascertain , whether In fart the workman had been served with notice of 
- ot . Moreover , he was not entitled to rely upon the alleged endorsements with 
out the persons, who had made the same being examined .

139. The contention on behalf of the workmen is, that this manner of 
recording the statements is highly objectionable , and that this has also prejudiced 


6B0. 3(1 )] 


THE GAZETTE OF INDIA : OCTOBER 29 , 1960 /KARTIK 7, 1882 


3243 


them . Granting, that the enquiry by a domestic tribunals is not of the same 
nature, as that in a criminal case , and that the Evidence Act has no application 
strictly to the enquiries conducted by tribunals , even though they may be quasi 
Judicial in character , still the law requires , that such tribunals should observo 
the rules of natural justice in the conduct of the enquiry . The rules of natural 
justice require , that a party should have the opporiunity of adduc.ng all relevant 
evidence , on which he relies , that the evidence of the opponent should be taken 
in his presence, and that he should be given an opportunity of cross - examining 
witnesses examined by the opposite party , and that no materials should be 
relied upon against him , without his being given an opportunity of explaining 
them . I these rules are satisfied , then the enquiry is not open to attack on the 
ground , that the procedure laid down in the Evidence Act , for taking evidence, 
was not strictly followed . This was the view taken by the Supreme Court in 
New Prakash Transport Company ( 1957 S .C .R . 98 ) . On the facts of the present 
the workmen have been prejudiced by the manner , in which the Enquiry Officer 
took down the statements. When they have been recorded in the third person , 
It is not possible to hold , that the statements ascribed to the witnesses were in 
tact the statements made by them . In my opinion , the rules of natural justice 
require , that the statements of the witnesses, examined against the delinquents 
should be in their own language, otherwise objection might be taken , as rightly 
too , that the statement written down is not that of the witness making the 
statement, but thai of the Enquiry Officer . In other words, the statement 
becomes not of the witness , but of the Enquiry Omcer . When a statement is 
not in the first person , it would be scarcely appropriate to call it a statement 
of the witness himself. It the statement of the witness is not taken as a whole , 
and as far as possible in the words of the person examined , there is eminent 
danger , that statements favourable to the accused persons are omitted . An 
abstract of the statement of a witness cannot be deemed to be evidence against 
the accused person . In view of the above circumstances , the criticism has been 
rightly put forward, that what is relied upon aga nst the workmen in ques .ion 
is not what the witnesses of the Bank stated against them , but. what Shri Sarwal 
ftated , was stated by them , and that this is against the principles of natural 
Justice

146 . The next contention , that has been raised , is with reference to tho 
findings dated 17th June , 1959 . At the outset, there can be no doubt at all, 
that these findings were not in existence on 17th June, the date, which they 
bear. They were prepared later in collaboration with legal advisers . No copies 
of these findings were delivered to the workmen , It has been held , that omission 
to deliver copies of the Andings is a serious infirmity . In the decision in 
Chowdhry Vs. Union of India ( 1957 I LLJ 494 ) , already referred to the rules 
of natural justice have been set out. The Enquiry Omcer must be careful to 
deal with the charges, as framed and not depart from them , and import extra 
neous matter. The delinquent is entitled to a copy of the finding or the report 
of the Enquiry Tribunal, and , without the same, the deliquent can never show 
cause against the propped punishment. The same view was taken in the . 
decision in Persadi and Company Vs. Works Manager , Eastern Railway ( 1957 I 
LLJ 272 ) . It is important to note that in the letter dated 17th June, 1959, the 
Enquiry Officer called upon the six workmen to show causc in mitigation of 
punishment. Without a copy of the findings, it was certainly not possible for 
them to comply with this demand . In fact, as already indicated , they did ask 
for a copy of the finding by thcir letters deted 20th June, 1959, and subsequently , 
but no copies were ever supplicd , and the Enquiry Officer took the vicw , that 
he was not bound to supply the same under the Award . Apparently cven on 
23rd June. 1959, the Enquiry Officer rend nut from some pencil notes, which 
were not on record . The hoaring was concluded and the workmen were punish 
ed on 23rd Junc, 1959, with the punishment of dismissal, and the enquiry 
concluded there, and I have given my reasons for holding, that the subsequent 
proceedings dated 24 / 27th June . 1959, are only meant to cover up the defects 
in the proceedings . The principles , laid down in the declsion , referred to above, 
apply to the facts of this case . Also in the Sastry Award in Paragraph 521 ( 10 ) 
ir is stated as follows: -- - 
" He will also be permitted to be defended by a representative of a 

registered trade union of bank employees, or with the bank s 
permission by a lawyer . He shall also he given a hearing, as regards 
the nature of the proposer punishment, in case any charges aro 
established against them ." In view of the last requirement, the 
workmen were entitled to a copy of the findings , under which the 
Enquiry Omirer found that the charges were established against 
them . Withou : this they could not plead in mitigation of punishment 
especially when they were ignorant of what had been found against 
them . This was evnn worse when the nature of the proposed punish 
ment was not disclosed in the lotier dated 17th June, 1959. The 
enquiry is vitiatrd by the omission to supply a copy of the findings , 
and this has deprived the workmen of the valuable right of making 

thelr defence before they were dismissed from service."

155 . It is contended on behalf of the workmen , that a perusal of the Andings 
will show , that the Enquiry Officer held them guilty , not only on the basis of 


Sec . $( li)] 


THE GAZETTE OF INDIA : OCTOBER 29, 1960 / KARTIK 7, 1882 


3247 


what the Bank s witnesses are alleged to have stated with reference to the alle 
gations in the charge - sheets , on the days mentioned therein , but also that he had 
taken into account the events alleged to have taken place on 10th April, 1959, 
and even thereafter , as proving their guilt. In other words, it is contended, that 
he has imported extraneous matters into the findings , i. e ., events alleged to have 
taken place subs - quent to the dates mentioned in the charge -sheets , and also 
events based on his own personal knowledge , and that this is particularly objec 
tionable in view of his ruling, ( Ext . M / 4B , M /5A , and M /BÅ ) , that he would 
not allow any subsequent events to be tuken into account, and that the workmen 
should confine themseives to the events on the dates mentioned in the charge 
sheets. It is dißcult to resist the contention on behalf of the workmen , that tho 
Enquiry Officer , in holding the workmen guilty of the charges against them , has 
imported his own personal knowl dge, and extraneous considerations, and evi 
dence about subsequent events. This is objectionable , and vitiates the entire 
proceedings , In the decision in B . C . Chaterjee Vs. State of Bengal ( 1954 II LLJ 
713 ) it was held by the Calcutta High Court , that, although a departmental en 
quiry need not be carried out strictly according to the rules applicable to judicial 
proceedings, still the principles of natural justice should be followed , and that it 
is one of the fundamental principles of natural justice, that a judge cannot give 
evidence before himself, and weigh the same, while coming to the decision . It 
was also found , that the Enquiry Omcer therein was in fact a material witness , 
and that he should equally and promptly remove himself from the office of the 
Enquiry Tribunal. On the facts of the present case, it has been established on 
the evidence of Shri Sarwal, that he was not only the Enquiry Officer, but also 
the r . presentative of the Bank , who framed the charge - sheets on the basis of 
events, that were within his own personal knowledge . There is no doubt, that 
Shri Sarwal has imported his own personal knowledge and extraneous matters 
in arriving at the finding of guilt, and this vitiates the finding. It is laid down 
in Syed Qamar Ali and State of Madhya Pradesh ( 1959 I LLJ 47) by the Madhya 
Pradesh High Court, that, when an order of dismissal is partly based on admissible 
material, and partly on inadmissible material, which could not be separated, the 
entire order must be held to be void , and inoperative . 

156. It was observed in Lala Laxman Day Vs. Goverdhan Das ( A . I. R . 1960 
Punjab 8 ) , that in an enquiry before the domestic tribunal, the person , who is 
charged , is entitled to know the conclusions arrived at by the punishing autho 
rity , for proposing the punishment in order that he may be in a position to defend 
his case . The mire service of notice of the proposed punishment cannot be 
deemed to be giving a reasonable opportunity to the public servant for showing 
cause against the action proposed to be taken in regard to him . The service of the 
copy of the findings of the Punishing Authority on the public servant is mandatory . 
In the decision in Dhian Singh Vs. D - puty Secretary Government of Punjab ( 1960 
Punjab 41) , it was laid down , that the Tribunal concerned , must consciously 
Batis y itself, before proceeding to hear and dispose of the case ex - parte or in 
default, that a notice of the enquiry in fact has been duly served . This is one 
of the basic rules of Indian Justice. There is no doubt at all that the principles 
laid down above have not been kept in mind by the Enquiry Oncer . The find 
ings arrived at, are baseless and perverse , and cannot be up -held . 


157. A consideration of the various circumstances in the cose establish , that 
the enquiry held on behalf of the bank by Shri Sarwal is neither fair nor im 
partial. It is vitiated by infringement of the principles of natural justice. He 
was a person who was biased against the workmen and he being the Enquiry 
Officer for conducting the enguiry , and punishing the worlcmen ing in bad faith . 
More especially is this so , when it is clear from the record , that he was only the 
mouth pice of the manager . It is difficult to believe that Mr. Sarwal kept an 
open mind, and , on the contrary , there is no doubt, that he had no option except 
to toe the line of Mr. Brown and the management. There is no doubt, that the 
charge - sheets were served against these six workmen , not becaug . the charges 
were true but because they were the office begrers and active members of the 
Union , and they were responsible primarily for putting - forward the rights of the 
workmen as against the management. I find , that the action taken against the 
workmen is vitiated by bad faith , that there has been violation of the principleg 
of natural justice , that the charges against the workmen are not true, that the 
findings of the Enquiry Oficer are perverse and baseless , and that the action 
taken by the management against the six workmen in question is an act of victimi 
sation and unfair labour practice . 


158. In view of my Andings , as above, the order of dismissal passed agalngt 
them is wrongful and unjustified , and cannot be permitted to stand . Accordingly , 
It is set aside,

(PART IT 


- - - -

159 . With reference to the question of relief , I see no sufficient grounds for 
denying the reliet of rc - instatement. The argument, that these workmen had 
hehayd in a dis -urderly manner , and that they were ring leaders , is not entitled 
to wly weight, und they are entitled to re- instatement. They are entitled to back 
wages also . 

180 . The contention has been put - torward on behalf of the workmen , that 
they are also entitled to wages for the strike period . This is wholly outside the 
scope of this reference , and it cannot be entertained in this procceding. I fud , 
that the dismissal of the six workmen in question is wrongful, and unjustified . 
and that they are entitled to re - instatement, and also back wages, as claimed . 
Issues No. 4 and 9 


161. The contention has been raised on behalf of the workmen , that an enquiry 
should be directed against the Manager , and Shri Sarwal, under Paragraph 517 
of the Sastry Award . No doubt, there is provision for an enquiry in the Sastry 
Award . In paragraph 517 ( 2 ) the proviso is to the effect, that complaints relating 
to assault, or abuse by any person holding a supervisory position , shall be cnquir 
ed into immediately . In vicw of the fact that 21 workmen preferred a complaint 
of abuso and wrongful confinement by Mr. Brown and Shiri Sarwal, under this 
provision it was incumbent upon the Bank to order an enquiry into this com 
plaint. The Head Office at London failed to order an enquiry , in spite of being 
requesied to do so . This is an infringement of the terms of the Bank Award . 

162. However , granting , that an enquiry was called for, on the facts of this 
cane, I do not feel called upon to order any such enquiry . It is sufficient in the 
Interests of justice , that the workmen are re -instated in service , and restored to 
their old posts , together with back wages . 
Issue No. 10 
163. In the result an award is passed as follows: 
( i ) The order of dismissal passed against the six workmen in question by 

the management of the National & Grindlays Bank Limited , is set 
aside, as being wrongful, and unjustified and the said management 
shall re - instate the six workmen in service , and restore them to their 
old posts on the same terms and conditions of service, as before, 
within two weeks from the date , when thls award becomes enforce 
able , and the period between the date of dismissal and the date of 
rc-instatement shall not operate a break in continuity of service , 

and the said period shall be treated as continuous service . 
( II) The said management shall also pay to each of the afore-mentioned 

workmen all the back wages , and emoluments due to them at the 
rate , at which they were being paid to him at the time of dismissal, 

froin the date of dismissal to the date of re - instaterncnt. 
(ili ) Thera will be no order as to costs .

Agreement;
